<a id='question-150'></a>

### Question 150: Immunosuppressant Complication 50: Azathioprine / 6-MP Toxic Accumulation with Allopurinol Xanthine Oxidase Co-administration
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Azathioprine (AZA) and 6-mercaptopurine (6-MP) are prodrugs metabolized by xanthine oxidase (XO) to their active forms. Co-administration with XO inhibitors (e.g., allopurinol) increases AZA/6-MP levels, leading to severe myelosuppression. (阿硫唑嘌呤 (AZA) 和 6-巯基嘌呤 (6-MP) 是前药，由黄嘌呤氧化酶 (XO) 代谢成活性形式。 与 XO 抑制剂 (例如别嘌呤醇) 联合用药会增加 AZA/6-MP 水平，导致严重的骨髓抑制。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 58-year-old male with a history of Crohn's disease, status post ileocecal resection 5 years ago, is being treated with azathioprine 150 mg daily for maintenance therapy. He also has a history of gout, for which he takes allopurinol 300 mg daily. Over the past month, he has experienced increasing fatigue, recurrent fevers, and oral ulcers. His complete blood count (CBC) reveals severe leukopenia (WBC 1,200/µL, absolute neutrophil count 200/µL), thrombocytopenia (platelets 30,000/µL), and macrocytic anemia (Hgb 9.5 g/dL, MCV 110 fL). A peripheral blood smear shows hypersegmented neutrophils. Bone marrow biopsy reveals hypocellularity with decreased myeloid precursors. Azathioprine and 6-thioguanine nucleotide (6-TGN) levels are markedly elevated. Which of the following mechanisms best explains this patient's presentation?

(A) Allopurinol competitively inhibits the uptake of azathioprine into intestinal cells, reducing its bioavailability and leading to therapeutic failure.
(B) Allopurinol inhibits the enzyme hypoxanthine-guanine phosphoribosyltransferase (HGPRT), preventing the salvage of purines and leading to decreased levels of active azathioprine metabolites.
(C) Allopurinol inhibits xanthine oxidase, the enzyme responsible for converting azathioprine to its active metabolite 6-mercaptopurine (6-MP), leading to decreased 6-MP levels and reduced immunosuppression.
(D) Allopurinol inhibits xanthine oxidase, the enzyme responsible for converting 6-MP to its inactive metabolite 6-thiouric acid, leading to accumulation of active 6-MP and its toxic effects.
(E) Azathioprine directly inhibits xanthine oxidase, leading to decreased uric acid levels and exacerbating the patient's gout symptoms, while also causing bone marrow suppression.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 58-year-old male with Crohn's disease (requiring immunosuppression) and gout (requiring urate-lowering therapy).
- **Medications:** Azathioprine (AZA) for Crohn's, Allopurinol for gout. This combination is a classic setup for a drug interaction.
- **Symptoms:** Fatigue, fevers, oral ulcers – suggestive of immunosuppression failure or toxicity.
- **Lab Findings:** Severe leukopenia (especially neutropenia), thrombocytopenia, macrocytic anemia, hypersegmented neutrophils (suggestive of megaloblastic changes, often seen with 6-MP toxicity).
- **Bone Marrow:** Hypocellularity with decreased myeloid precursors – consistent with severe myelosuppression.
- **Specific Levels:** Elevated azathioprine and 6-thioguanine nucleotide (6-TGN) levels – confirms accumulation of the active metabolite.
- **Question:** Asks for the mechanism explaining the presentation.

The key is the combination of AZA and allopurinol, leading to severe myelosuppression and elevated active metabolite levels. This points directly to an interaction involving the metabolism of AZA/6-MP.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **Azathioprine Metabolism:** Azathioprine is a prodrug. It is converted non-enzymatically to 6-mercaptopurine (6-MP). 6-MP is then metabolized via three main pathways:
    1.  **Xanthine Oxidase (XO):** Converts 6-MP to 6-thiouric acid (inactive).
    2.  **Hypoxanthine-Guanine Phosphoribosyltransferase (HGPRT):** Converts 6-MP to 6-thioguanine nucleotides (6-TGNs), which are the active cytotoxic metabolites responsible for immunosuppression and myelosuppression.
    3.  **Thiopurine S-methyltransferase (TPMT):** Converts 6-MP to 6-methylmercaptopurine (inactive).
- **Allopurinol Mechanism:** Allopurinol is a potent inhibitor of xanthine oxidase. It is used to treat gout by reducing uric acid production.
- **Interaction:** When allopurinol is co-administered with azathioprine, it inhibits the XO pathway of 6-MP metabolism. This leads to:
    1.  Decreased conversion of 6-MP to the inactive 6-thiouric acid.
    2.  Increased levels of 6-MP.
    3.  Increased substrate availability for the HGPRT pathway.
    4.  Increased formation of the active cytotoxic metabolite, 6-TGNs.
    5.  Accumulation of 6-TGNs leads to severe myelosuppression (leukopenia, thrombocytopenia) and other toxicities.
- **Patient Presentation:** The patient's severe pancytopenia, macrocytic anemia with hypersegmented neutrophils (characteristic of 6-MP toxicity), and elevated 6-TGN levels are all consistent with this drug interaction.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Incorrect. Allopurinol inhibits XO, not uptake. Inhibiting uptake would *decrease* AZA/6-MP levels, leading to therapeutic failure, not toxicity.
- (B): Incorrect. Allopurinol inhibits XO, not HGPRT. HGPRT is involved in the *activation* of 6-MP to 6-TGNs. Inhibiting HGPRT would decrease active metabolites.
- (C): Incorrect. Allopurinol inhibits XO, which *converts* 6-MP to its inactive metabolite. Inhibiting this pathway leads to *increased* 6-MP levels, not decreased.
- (D): Correct. Allopurinol inhibits XO, preventing the inactivation of 6-MP. This leads to accumulation of 6-MP and its active metabolite 6-TGN, causing severe myelosuppression. This perfectly explains the clinical picture.
- (E): Incorrect. Azathioprine is metabolized *to* 6-MP, it doesn't directly inhibit XO. While XO inhibition does lower uric acid, the primary clinical consequence here is myelosuppression due to AZA/6-MP accumulation, not gout exacerbation.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature             | Azathioprine/6-MP Metabolism | Allopurinol Mechanism | Interaction Effect                                  | Clinical Consequence                               |
| :------------------ | :--------------------------- | :-------------------- | :------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------- |
| Key Enzyme          | Xanthine Oxidase (XO)        | Xanthine Oxidase (XO) | Inhibition of XO by Allopurinol                   | Increased 6-MP & 6-TGN levels                       |
| Metabolite Fate     | 6-MP -> 6-Thiouric Acid (inactive) | N/A                  | Decreased conversion of 6-MP to 6-Thiouric Acid    | Accumulation of active 6-TGNs                       |
| Active Metabolite   | 6-Thioguanine Nucleotides (6-TGNs) | N/A                  | Increased formation of 6-TGNs                     | Severe myelosuppression (leukopenia, thrombocytopenia) |
| HGPRT Role          | 6-MP -> 6-TGNs (active)      | N/A                  | Increased substrate for HGPRT due to less inactivation | Increased active metabolite production               |
| Uptake              | Intestinal cells             | N/A                  | Not affected by Allopurinol                         | N/A                                                 |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Azathioprine and 6-mercaptopurine are metabolized by xanthine oxidase. Co-administration with xanthine oxidase inhibitors like allopurinol or febuxostat significantly increases the levels of the active metabolite 6-TGN, leading to severe myelosuppression. This is a classic and high-yield drug interaction tested on USMLE Step 1. Differential diagnoses for myelosuppression include other drugs (chemotherapy, antibiotics), infections, autoimmune diseases, and intrinsic bone marrow disorders. However, the specific combination of AZA/6-MP and allopurinol strongly points to this interaction.
